The headline reproduces exactly: both reversal handlers end a failed partial with a `console.error` and `skipped: 'no_transition'`, no write — and the only other trace is `webhook_events`, whose three columns are `(provider, event_id, received_at)`, so nothing in the database relates the delivery to a ledger, an amount or an outcome. `event_orders` really does carry no refunded amount (`20261229_001`: `amount_cents`, `platform_fee_cents`, `refunded_at`, nothing else). **But `refunded_cents` was never accumulated**, and that sentence is the load-bearing error: `refundedCentsOfCharge` returns Stripe's own CUMULATIVE `charge.amount_refunded`, and the CAS carries `.lte('refunded_cents', …)`, so the column is a monotone LATCH on an externally-computed total with no `+=` anywhere. The choice was never accumulate-vs-derive; it was mirror-an-external-total vs sum-a-local-set. **And summing the local set is the one thing that had to be refused.** `charge.refunded` carries a Charge and no refund id, so no child row can be keyed off it; only the refund-lifecycle trio carries a Refund. A refund that succeeds instantly and emits no lifecycle event yields no child row at all, and which events an endpoint receives is dashboard configuration this repo cannot read (§ 789 measured that). Deriving the total from an incomplete set would count a succeeded refund with no row as money the charity kept — a bounded understatement traded for an unbounded OVERSTATEMENT on a public thermometer. So `refunded_cents` stays the mirror, and the child rows are the authority for REVERSALS only: `fundraiser_totals` / `fundraiser_feed` report `amount_cents - refunded_cents + least(reversed, refunded_cents)`, a correction that is strictly additive and clamped, and nothing new joins `derived_state.md` because nothing new is cached. **The direction of the error is also the opposite of what the entry implies**: the thermometer UNDERSTATES what the charity raised, which is § 789's "smaller, safe lie" — the money is ours while the ledger says it went out. Built: `payment_refunds` (migration `20270630000001`), one row per Stripe Refund, `stripe_refund_id` unique, exactly one parent ledger by CHECK, Stripe's own five-status vocabulary, `revoke all` from both client roles and no policy, plus a terminal-status latch in the write-lock trigger so an out-of-order `pending` cannot un-say a `refund.failed`. Idempotency is proved on the TOTAL rather than the row count, because the total is what a double-apply corrupts. **Two places the entry understated itself**: the worklist it asks to match does not exist for the full case either — `where status = 'refund_failed'` names the order but no amount, because the table has none — and "a schema change, not a webhook arm" is both, since nothing was writing a Refund id anywhere. Original entry: § 789 refuses to move `partially_refunded` on either ledger, for two good reasons (it is seat-bearing on orders; on donations the only alternative is non-idempotent arithmetic on `refunded_cents`). The cost is that the money discrepancy exists **only** in a `console.error`: `event_orders` carries no refunded amount at all, and `donations.refunded_cents` keeps stating an instalment that came back to us. Nothing queryable says so. The durable shape is probably a per-refund child row keyed on the Stripe Refund id — which is idempotent by construction, would let `refunded_cents` be *derived* rather than accumulated, and would give the partial case the same worklist the full case now has. Sized as a schema change, not a webhook arm.

- [x] **Which refund events reach us is dashboard configuration nothing in this repo records.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 826](../architecture/decisions.md)). The three events are now pre-deploy checklist items in [club_events.md](../features/club_events.md) beside the `sk_test_` / `ca_` / `whsec_` key items. The recording half had a better answer than a doc line: § 789's "this repo cannot read the delivery version" is true at *build* time and false at *run* time — every Stripe event payload carries `api_version`, and `parseStripeEventEnvelope` was throwing it away on arrival. It is now carried on the envelope, graded by `refundEventsApiEra` against `2024-10-28.acacia`, and logged on the refund branch beside the raw value, so a `legacy` line names an endpoint that is receiving only the deprecated `charge.refund.updated`. Compared on the ISO date prefix, because the `.name` suffix is a label and not an ordinal; an unparseable version claims `unknown`, never `modern`. **What it still cannot do** is see the endpoint's `enabled_events` list — an event that is not enabled is never delivered, so there is nothing to log and the failure mode stays the silent one. That half is unreachable from here in any form and is why the checklist entry exists.

## Surfaced by the #789 Edge Function vacuity sweep (2026-08-28)

- [x] **`donationStatusTransition — the scope default is full` is the one vacuous Edge Function test left standing.** **Closed 2026-08-28 on the round-24 integration branch**, where the two slices that could not both reach it met: § 788 measured it and left it in `EXPECTED_SURVIVORS` because `stripe-events-webhook` belonged to § 785, and § 785 rewrote the file without knowing it was vacuous. Both assertions now name `refunded` outright, and the `EXPECTED_SURVIVORS` entry is deleted in the same commit — its own staleness check fails the build otherwise, which is what made the two halves inseparable. The guard now runs with an empty exemption list, so the next survivor is a build failure rather than an entry. Original entry: Measured, not suspected: it is the single survivor of the mutation guard added in [decisions § 788](../architecture/decisions.md)arried. **Fix:** assert the pair equals `'refunded'` (the value the very next test in the file already pins independently, so nothing new is being claimed). It is filed rather than fixed only because `stripe-events-webhook` was owned by a different change in the same round. It is listed by name in `EXPECTED_SURVIVORS` in `apps/backend/scripts/check_edge_function_test_vacuity.mjs`; that entry's staleness check **fails the build** once the test is fixed, so closing this item means deleting the entry in the same commit.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 815](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **The 23 `handler_envelope.test.ts` integration tests are outside every mutation operator this repo has.** The gap was exactly as described and is now closed by `apps/backend/scripts/check_served_envelope_mutations.mjs`, which mutates the tree the HOST is serving one gate at a time and requires the case naming that gate to fail: **23 cases, 20 mutations, 24 declared kills, 0 survived, 0 unmeasured**, wired into the `edge-functions` job the aggregator already waits on. **The filing was wrong three ways.** The count is **22**, not 23 — the 23rd `Deno.test` is a placeholder with `ignore: true` and an empty body, added so `deno test` prints an ignored case instead of "no tests"; "23" is deno's *ignored* count, which includes it. The price was wrong: `functions serve` re-reads a changed module on the next request (measured under 1.4 s), so there is **no second mutant tree and no second host boot** — 58–69 s in the job that already booted everything. And "nothing suggests they are vacuous" was false for **three of them**: the bare-GET secret case was being answered 403 by the verify-token gate whatever the secret gate did; the verify-token gate had **no case at all**, so an attacker with the URL secret could complete a subscription handshake unobserved; and the non-create case asserted a 200 `OK` that the not-connected-athlete branch returns verbatim, so deleting the early return it exists to prove left it green. All three are fixed and each was a measured survivor first. A bystander found the same way: the job's `docker stop supabase_edge_runtime_backend` had never stopped anything — containers are named for `project_id`, which is `project-running`. **One defect in the operator itself surfaced only in real CI** and is fixed in the same closure: the round's reload wait accepted any probe answer that merely *differed* from the pre-mutation one, and `functions serve` restarts the runtime around a file change, so two consecutive gateway 503s let a round run against a host serving nothing — scoring its kills as killed and its **spares as moved**. Each mutation now declares the answer its mutant settles on and the wait requires that answer byte-identically twice; a gateway status may only be declared with a body no restart can produce.

## Surfaced by the #789 shared-constant sweep (2026-08-28)

Found by the class sweep behind [decisions § 787](../architecture/decisions.md). Four divergences in that round were fixed and registered in `scripts/check_shared_constants.mjs`; these are the ones that need their own change.

- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 790](../architecture/decisions.md)) **Exercise-name normalisation disagreed between SQL and both clients, on a PERSISTED grouping key.** `public.normalise_exercise_name` (migration `20270623000001`) is now the single SQL definition, written by explicit code point, and the four live RPCs call it; both clients respell the same class so one parser reads all three, and the class is registered in `check_shared_constants.mjs` (mutation-verified). Two `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE` CHECK constraints make the stored key's canonicity the database's invariant, behind an idempotent batched backfill. **What the filing got wrong:** the count was **four** live functions, not seven — `20261225_001`, `20261231_001` and `20270323_001` hold definitions a later `create function` replaced, which is exactly the read-one-migration error § 787 records. Gap (a) held exactly. Gap (b) was wrong on three of the four characters it named, in the direction that understated the problem: measured on the live stack (PG 17.6, ICU provider) `\s` **matches** U+00A0, U+2007 and U+202F — only U+FEFF is excluded — and it also matches U+001C-U+001F, which neither client folds and the filing never mentions. The real defect was one layer deeper than either gap: `\s` is `[[:space:]]`, whose membership past ASCII is the collation's opinion (`chr(160) ~ '\s'` is true under the database default and false under `collate "en_US.utf8"`), so the four copies disagreed with **themselves across deployments**. And no backfill was needed: 0 of 47 persisted key rows diverged, because nothing in SQL ever wrote either column — both are stamped client-side, so the clients were always the persisting rail and the anchor. The shared fixture was extended as the filing suggested.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 791](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **The Dart rail of the CHECK-constraint class is guarded by nothing.** `check_constraint_unions.mjs` now reads Dart: **29 distinct declarations across 22 files** are registered as rails (enums read as snake_case, const string lists, `<String, …>` key maps, record lists), and a widened CHECK fails the Dart rail as loudly as the web one — proved by widening `routes.surface` in its migration and watching all three of its rails fail. **The filing undercounted: it said nineteen and its own enumeration lists twenty; measured, 29 do.** It missed `nutrition_totals.dart` `mealSlots`, `run_stats.dart` `_pbBracketLabels`, `export_job.dart` `_statusTokens`, `recurrence.dart` `RecurrenceFreq`, `recap_screen.dart` `RecapPeriod`, `preferences.dart` `DistanceUnit`, and all three of `routine_builder_sheet.dart`'s option lists. It also offered a choice of fix ("either extend `check_constraint_unions.mjs` … or register the ones that are real vocabularies in `check_shared_constants.mjs`") — the second would have split one class across two registries, which is the defect § 787 recorded, so the first was taken. Rails name `mobile_android` only and a test pins that its `mobile_ios` twin is byte-identical.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 791](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **`check_constraint_unions.mjs` covers 41 of 68 set-shaped CHECK columns.** The registry is now keyed on the COLUMN rather than on a union name and holds **all 67 live columns and 121 client rails (87 web, 34 Dart) across 46 files**; coverage is enforced in the guard, so a new set-shaped CHECK column fails until it is registered — with rails, or with an empty `clients` list and a `note` saying no client enumerates it (15 of the 67). **41 was exact; 68 was the raw replay count and 67 is the live one** — the phantom the filing named in its own last clause (`runs.kind`) is now excluded by reading `database.types.ts` for the live column set, and *registering* one fails. **"Eleven" was a large undercount: 37 declarations across 23 files live outside `types.ts`.** The two deliberate deltas the filing asked for a mode for are declared per rail as `allowExtra` / `allowMissing` rather than as a blanket wider/narrower mode — a blanket mode would tolerate the NEXT client-only value too — and each exemption is itself checked for staleness. `reports.status` turned out to need no exemption: its two resolved statuses are spelled inline with no named vocabulary, so it is one of the 15. **Not addressed here** (out of this guard's scope, still open): the two `not valid` caps from `20261124_001` that were never validated, and `text_limits.test.ts`'s VALIDATE case reading only `20270502_001`.
- [ ] **Documented firmware counts are transcriptions, and five of them had rotted at once.** [§ 793](../architecture/decisions.md) fixed a stale GATT characteristic count in five files, a settings-version range in two, a `face::Metric` count, three `Page`-ring counts and a settings-menu row count — every one of them a number a doc restates about a Rust `enum` or `const` that nothing compares. The wire versions and frame caps are now guarded by `scripts/check_watch_wire_vectors.mjs`, but that guard reads *code against code*; nothing reads a doc's number against the enum it describes. The shape that would close it: a small registry of `(file, phrase, symbol)` — "nine characteristics" ↔ the `#[characteristic(` count in `ble.rs`, "41 built-ins" ↔ `Page` variants less the four `Screen*`, "eight rows" ↔ `MENU_ITEMS`, "40 variants" ↔ `face::Metric` — failing when the prose and the source part. Not done here because the phrasings are prose and a matcher over them is its own design problem; the alternative worth weighing first is writing every such count **count-free** (as `settings_menu.rs`'s "one checkbox per page" now is), which needs no guard at all.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 816](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **`scripts/comment_strip.mjs` cannot lex Dart `${…}` interpolation, so three test files are unreadable to every guard built on it.** `${` now opens a nested CODE region the lexer scans as code — its own strings (any quote style, raw or not), its own nested braces, its own further interpolations, its own comments (blanked, because a comment in a code region is a comment), and newlines regardless of the enclosing quote style. `r`-prefixed strings stay one verbatim span, since `$` is literal in them. An interpolation that never closes throws `unterminated string interpolation`, keeping § 793's rule that source a guard cannot read is source it must not report a verdict about. § 793's workaround is gone: `check_watch_wire_vectors.mjs` reads **every** Dart file under `apps/mobile_android/{lib,test}` — 1056 of them, up from the **7** the magic filter let through, with 514 test files skipped outright — and an unlexable file is now a hard error unconditionally rather than only when it carries a magic. **What the filing got wrong, in three places.** It said *three test files*; measured, **8 committed Dart files** could not be lexed — four distinct paths doubled by the byte-identical iOS twin — and one of the four is **library code, not a test**: `apps/mobile_android/lib/import_failures.dart`, whose CSV quoter is `'"${value.replaceAll('"', '""')}"'`. It said the newline case sits *inside a triple-quoted Dart string*; measured, `coach_screen_helpers_test.dart`'s is inside a **single-quoted** one — Dart permits a newline inside `${…}` whatever the enclosing quotes, which is strictly harder than what was filed, and that file also nests a brace-carrying map literal in the region. And it framed the workaround as merely narrow rather than structurally blind: the filter admitted a file only if one of the firmware's 16 wire magics appeared as hex *in its bytes*, so a golden spelled payload-only — or with its magic written `[0x43, 0x52, …]` — would have sat outside the sweep with nothing to say so. What it got right: the lexer tracked no code region at all, nothing was silently unchecked under the workaround, the fix belonged in the lexer, and `comment_strip.test.mjs` was the place for the cases. **Removing the workaround did find something**: with the magic filter gone, `status_color_literal_guard_test.dart`'s `_bannedHexes` — 21 six-digit colour literals in a list — joined into one 63-byte "vector" the registry had never heard of, so `dartHexConsts` now refuses a collection-shaped declaration (on the bracket, not on the comma: the registered `_goldenBlob` is `_hex('…' '…',)`, and the guard's own suite caught a comma rule that dropped it). Verified: **1969 of 1969** committed Dart files lex, up from 1961; across all **2129** Dart + Rust files the new lexer's output is byte-identical to the old one wherever the old one ran, **0** differences, and idempotent everywhere. **Left open:** the 14 `otherRail` reads over 6 Swift / Kotlin / TypeScript / Monkey C files are still taken from **raw, unlexed** source, so a commented-out declaration there can still answer for a live one — § 773's class, three languages over. Measured as latent today, not live: the nearest misses are `WatchIngestBridge.swift`'s doc comment naming `ArmedRoute.maxPoints` while that file's rail reads `maxRoutePoints`, and `grade_adjusted_pace.ts`'s `[MIN_SEGMENT_M]` in prose, which `tsConst` does not match. Closing it means teaching the lexer Swift's `\(…)`, Kotlin's `${…}` + `"""`, and TS template literals — a round of its own.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 817](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **Four CHECK vocabularies are spelled as INLINE unions, so no registry can name them.** The premise was exactly right — all four ARE enumerated by a client, anonymously, and each is now one exported type: `FinisherStatus` and `RaceSessionStatus` in `types.ts`, `ReportResolution` beside `ReportReason` in `data.ts`, `CoachMessageRole` in `coach/types.ts`, all four registered and green. **Every count in the filing was low.** `finisher_status` is written out **five** times in full (`core/data.ts` twice, `runs/event_results_csv.ts` three times), not four — the filing's own enumeration contradicted its headline — plus the `'dnf' | 'dns'` subset in `recordNonFinish`, now derived as `Exclude<FinisherStatus, 'finished'>` rather than re-typed. `reports.status` is **three** call sites, not two (`data.ts` plus two in `admin/reports/+page.svelte`). `coach_messages.role` is **six** spellings across three files, of which "every provider signature" is a third — the rest are `CoachRequestBody` and three inside `CoachChat.svelte`. Only `race_sessions.status` was counted correctly, at one. **Sixteen spellings over seven files, not the four/one/two/two the filing implied.** "About four lines apiece" holds for three of them: `reports.status` needs a staleness-checked `allowMissing: ['pending']` because the column admits three values and the moderation queue may only ever write two — a plain rail would have failed the guard on arrival. Each of the four is pinned by widening its real CHECK in memory and asserting the rail is what fails. Original filing below.
  - **Four CHECK vocabularies are spelled as INLINE unions, so no registry can name them.** `event_results.finisher_status` (`'finished' | 'dnf' | 'dns'`, written out four times — `core/data.ts` twice, `runs/event_results_csv.ts` three times, and a `recordNonFinish(status: 'dnf' | 'dns')` parameter), `race_sessions.status` (`'armed' | 'running' | 'finished' | 'cancelled'` on the race-session row type), `reports.status` (`'reviewed' | 'dismissed'` at two call sites) and `coach_messages.role` (`'user' | 'assistant'` in every provider signature) are all registered in `check_constraint_unions.mjs` as `clients: []` because there is no declaration to point at — they are the only four of the 15 no-client entries where a client genuinely DOES enumerate the column, just anonymously. Naming each as one exported type and registering it brings them under the guard for about four lines apiece. [decisions § 791](../architecture/decisions.md).
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 818](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **The Dart side has no `Record<Union, X>`, so its exhaustive lookups are unguarded by construction.** The premise and both fix shapes were right; **the size was out by a factor of fourteen.** A sweep of the whole `apps/mobile_android/lib` tree found **50** distinct switch sites over a CHECK-constrained string column, not 4 — **24** naming their column's whole current vocabulary and **26** already stopping short of it. Four are coincidence rather than enumeration and are deliberately unregistered (`onboarding.dart`'s `planPresetForGoal` switches over primary-goal answers that happen to be spelled like PR brackets; `activity_timeline_list.dart`'s three read the `activities` view's own `kind` literal, which shares `run`/`lift`/`meal` with `notifications.activity_kind` without being it). The other **46 are registered across 30 files and 22 columns**, taking the Dart half from 29 declarations over 22 files to **75 over 45** and the registry from 121 rails to **171**. Of the four sites the filing named, only `_verbFor` is a full-vocabulary lookup — the three progression switches name **five of six** and let `none` fall through, which is correct and is now declared as a staleness-checked `allowMissing` (22 of the 46 carry one). **The const-list option was refused**: a list beside the switch is a second declaration nothing checks against it — the § 641 shape, a copy whose divergence is undetectable — and would have meant editing 46 production call sites. The new `switch` shape reads the labels out of the switch itself, addressed by the innermost ENCLOSING declaration (optionally class-qualified), refusing ambiguity; **no production Dart changed.** Two implementation traps worth the reader's time are in the ADR: length-preserving comment blanking, so a doc line cannot become an anchor, and a balanced-paren declaration scan, because Dart `{named}` parameter groups made `_verbFor` — the widest switch in the tree — invisible to the first draft. Left open: coverage is enforced per COLUMN, so a *new* Dart switch over an already-registered column is still not forced to register. Original filing below.
  - **The Dart side has no `Record<Union, X>`, so its exhaustive lookups are unguarded by construction.** § 791 deliberately does not register web's icon / label maps: `Record<ChallengeMetric, string>` and its ~12 siblings are made exhaustive by `tsc`, so registering them would guard the compiler's own work. Dart has no analogue — `profile_screen.dart`'s `_verbFor` switches over all 16 `notifications.kind` values with a `default:` fallback, and `gym_session_screen.dart` / `routine_detail_screen.dart` / `gym_detail_screen.dart` do the same over `gym_routine_exercises.progression`. Each is correct today (verified) and each would degrade SILENTLY to the generic branch the day its CHECK gains a value, because a Dart `switch` over `String` cannot be exhaustive. The registry's `strings` shape cannot read a switch; either the switches key off a registered const list, or a shape that reads `case '<value>':` labels is added.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 830](../architecture/decisions.md)) **The exercise key's case-folding half was collation-dependent.** `public.normalise_exercise_name` pins `collate "und-x-icu"` (migration `20270630000001`), the ICU root locale, which outranks the argument's own collation. Verified over all 1,112,063 assignable code points and a 10,031-string context corpus under six collations: **zero** disagreement, where the old derivation disagreed on 14 / 24 / 10 / 9,849 of those strings under `tr-TR` / `lt-LT` / libc / `C`. Cost 0.47 µs a call against 0.34 µs, unchanged for non-ASCII input. **What the filing got wrong, and it is the half about the clients:** "Postgres `lower(U+0130)` returns `i` + U+0307 … matching JS and Dart" is right about JS and **wrong about Dart**, which returns a bare `i` — Dart implements Unicode SIMPLE case mapping from an older table where JS implements full mapping from a newer one, and measured over every code point the two **disagree at 466**, a divergence between the halves of a registered parity pair that no rail could see. It was reachable: for `İtme`, mobile's key failed § 790's CHECK with a **23514 on a legitimate save**, one of 412 such code points. The blast radius the filing asked for is 1 code point between the two providers, 2 between ICU `en-US` and `tr-TR` (one of them **ASCII `I`** — `lower('I')` is U+0131 on a Turkish database), 3 under `lt-LT`, 1,406 under `C`, plus the contextual Final_Sigma rule a per-code-point sweep cannot see. U+0130 and U+03C2 are now folded **by name** on all three rails and registered in `check_shared_constants.mjs` (mutation-verified). **The option the filing named as the durable fix was measured and deferred, not skipped** — see the entry below.
- [x] **`revoke execute ... from public` has never withheld a function from `anon`, and 83 migrations say it does.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 827](../architecture/decisions.md)). **The class was real and is already repaired** — `20270625000001` + `20270626000001`, the `check_migration_function_revoke_noop.mjs` statement guard and the `anon_execute_contract_test.sql` / `anon_execute_registry_test.sql` state guards landed on the round-26 integration branch ([§ 799](../architecture/decisions.md), [§ 800](../architecture/decisions.md)) before this round opened. This round **re-measured it independently instead of re-doing it**, and every figure reproduces: 91 violating statements over **69 routines** in **59 migrations**, split 90 `from public`-only / 1 `from anon`-only, and post-fix 242 `public` functions with exactly **34** non-trigger routines anon may execute — all 34 on the deliberate allowlist. **Three of the filing's four numbers are wrong, and its one reassurance is inverted.** 242 is exact. **83** counts neither routines (69) nor migrations (59) nor statements (91). **31** is 33 on the `revoke execute on function` reading (41 counting `revoke all`, 48 today). **186** was never true of the image it was measured on — 117 there — and is the CI/Cloud figure, where the model says 183. And *"most are SECURITY INVOKER with RLS beneath, so the stated intent failing is not automatically a reachable hole"* is backwards: **61 of the 69 are SECURITY DEFINER**, which bypasses RLS entirely, leaving **48 SECURITY DEFINER directly-callable functions** anon held EXECUTE on — the job queue, the mail batches, the shadow-hide evaluator, `cleanup_stale_rate_limits` (an anonymous call cleared every elapsed rate-limit window, defeating every throttle in the app). The remaining gap this round closed is documentary: the house rule now lives in `conventions.md` § *A revoke names every channel the grantee can reach the object through*, and `api_database.md`'s uncomputed "31" is now a computed 48. Guard re-proved by mutation in both directions. Original filing below. Surfaced on the round-25 integration branch by `normalise_exercise_name_test.sql`'s grant assertion ([decisions § 790](../architecture/decisions.md)). Supabase ships an `alter default privileges` for the `postgres` role in schema `public` that grants EXECUTE on every new function to `anon`, `authenticated` and `service_role`, so a function arrives with an **explicit** anon grant that revoking PUBLIC does not reach — [§ 781](../architecture/decisions.md)'s column-revoke no-op, one object class over. Measured against the live catalog: 242 functions in `public`, 186 carrying `anon=X`, **83 of them named in a migration that revoked them `from public`** (`claim_next_job`, `block_user`, `coach_roster_summary`, `enforce_create_rate_limit`, `admin_unhide_target` among them). The 31 migrations writing `from public, anon` are the house form and are unaffected. Each of the 83 needs its own judgement — most are SECURITY INVOKER with RLS beneath, so the stated intent failing is not automatically a reachable hole — plus the guard that would keep the answer, which is the `check_migration_column_revoke_noop.mjs` shape for functions. A round of its own.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 831](../architecture/decisions.md)) **`gym_exercise_names()` grouped on the spelling, not just preserved it.** It groups on `normalise_exercise_name` now (migration `20270630000004`) and `uses` counts the whole group, so a tab-prefixed paste and a case variant are one suggestion rather than three; the display stays case- and spelling-preserving. **What the filing got wrong:** it said to pick the most-used spelling "as `gym_exercise_records` already does with its `array_agg(display order by started_at desc, display)`" — read, that sibling picks the most **recent** spelling. The idiom is reused, the ordering is not, and the pgtap fixture is built so the two rules disagree (the most-used spelling is the older one), verified by mutating the `order by`. Two RPCs now answer the same display question by different rules; unifying them changes a shipped records surface and is filed below. A group whose only spelling carries edge whitespace still shows it — trimming the display would mean a fourth hand-written copy of the whitespace class, which is the defect § 790 exists to have removed.
- [ ] **The exercise key still depends on each runtime's Unicode version, and closing that needs `gym_sets.exercise_key` persisted first.** [§ 830](../architecture/decisions.md) removed the *collation* dependence; the *version* one is untouched and was measured: after the two named folds the three rails still disagree at **465** code points between web and mobile, **410** between mobile and the server, **55** between web and the server (Cherokee, Vithkuqi, Garay, Medefaidrin, Latin Extended-D/E, supplementary planes). Each is a name mobile cannot persist without a 23514 from `gym_routine_exercises_exercise_key_canonical`. The fix is the one § 790 used for whitespace — a frozen table of the 1,488 code points carrying a non-identity simple lowercase mapping, named on all three rails — and it is blocked on cost, not on doubt: `translate()` over that table costs **60 µs** a call against **0.34 µs** for `lower()` (200,000 calls: 12.0 s against 0.068 s), and the four RPCs re-derive the key once per `gym_sets` row, so a 15,000-set history would pay **~1.9 s of pure folding** per `gym_workout_summaries` call. An ASCII fast path leaves non-ASCII names at 62 µs, taxing exactly the population the table protects. Sequence it after persisting the key on `gym_sets` (a generated or trigger-maintained `exercise_key` + index) so nothing re-derives it at read time — a change to the shape of the highest-volume gym table, so `migration_locks.md` governs it. An ASCII-only fold is **not** the cheap alternative: 397 code points between U+00C0 and U+052F fold identically on all three rails today and it would stop folding every one, splitting "Überzüge" from "überzüge".
- [ ] **`gym_exercise_names` and `gym_exercise_records` pick a display spelling for the same key by different rules.** [§ 831](../architecture/decisions.md): the datalist picks the caller's most-**used** spelling, the records page the most-**recent** one, so the two surfaces can name one exercise differently. Both are defensible in isolation (a lifter who renamed "DB Bench" to "Dumbbell Bench Press" wants the new name on a records page and, for a while, the old one in an autocomplete they have used a hundred times) and neither is a key, so nothing is wrong downstream — but one question should have one rule. Unifying changes a shipped records surface, so decide the rule first.
- [x] **Three numeric caps hold different values on three rails, all conservative.** — **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 792](../architecture/decisions.md)). Three of the four are as filed and stay where they are: `club_posts.body` 1200 on exactly four surfaces vs 4096, `events.description` 1000 on two vs 2000, `training_plans.name` 80 on three vs 120. Being stricter than the column is a product decision, not a defect — a club post is a notice board and a plan name a header — so what changed is that each is written once instead of at nine call sites, with the containment now checked. **The parkrun one the entry called merely truncating is a reachable `23514`**: web carries no cap at all, not 30. The `maxlength="30"` on that page belongs to the runner handle, a different column. Both rails are now the column's own 32 — a parkrun id is `A` plus digits and approaches neither bound, and being stricter is exactly what silently truncated a 25-character id on the phone.
- [x] **Rate-limit *numbers* are unguarded; only the bucket vocabulary is registered.** — **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 792](../architecture/decisions.md)). Every figure verified by replay: eleven live call sites, eleven buckets, one-to-one, all ceilings exactly as filed. The two client doc comments no longer state the numbers — a transcription nothing reads was the whole complaint — and `api_database.md` gains a **bucket table** the guard parses, anchored on the backticked bucket name so it reads the row it means rather than the third number on a line. The guard also refuses something the prose could not express: **one bucket debited with two different ceilings**, which `check_rate_limit` cannot represent because it keys the counter by bucket alone. Not hypothetical — `create_report` has had four call sites over time and `clone_gym_routine_template` two; each resolves to one live site today, and the guard is what keeps it so.
- [x] **Watch-rail duplicates that are snapshot-pinned rather than compared.** **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 793](../architecture/decisions.md)). `scripts/check_watch_wire_vectors.mjs` reads every golden vector out of BOTH rails and compares them, plus a second registry for the values restated on more than one rail — the wire versions and frame caps, the Apple-Watch 512-point route budget across the three files that state it, the 40 m / 20 m off-route hysteresis across Rust / Dart / Swift / Kotlin, and the Minetti fit across Rust / Dart / TS / Monkey C. `LINK_SERVICE_UUID` went into `check_watch_ble_uuids.mjs`, where the service UUID's other two copies already live. **The filing was wrong twice.** It named five wire formats; there are six with paired goldens — it left out `TRK1`, the run blob, which is the only one that carries a finished run and the only one whose divergence loses data. And it said "every golden vector exists twice": `golden_vector_auto_lap_only` existed on the firmware rail alone, so both `flags3` fields were pinned on the phone only inside the 196-byte all-fields golden. It has a counterpart now. Measured first: all seventeen pairs agreed already, so nothing had drifted — the guard is a lock on a door that had not yet been tried. Proved by eight mutations, one per rule.
- [x] **Two watch docs state a firmware fact that is no longer true.** **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 793](../architecture/decisions.md)). Both claims were real, and both were understated. The stale "seven characteristics" was in **five** files, not one — `firmware.md`, `roadmap.md`, `privacy.md` (where it is a security claim), `quality_standards.md` and `local_testing.md`; `ble.rs` declares nine. `watch_settings.dart`'s comment was wrong at both ends, not just the top: `decode` accepts **v3 through v8**, so v1 and v2 no longer decode AND v8 was missing from the range the comment named. The sweep for the class found two more in `firmware.md` alone: it said the watch "records runs locally to LittleFS" (tier 1 has no filesystem — four 4 KiB internal-flash slots, 253 records), and it named `off_route_alert.dart` as the phone-side twin of the 40 m / 20 m thresholds when that module is the escalation-to-a-contact path and deliberately fires at 75 m / 90 s. `tier1_log.md` and § 377's own ADR text keep their counts: those are dated records, and this log rewrites no history. Four more of the class turned up in the same sweep and are fixed: `local_testing.md` said BTN3 walks "seven rows" of the settings menu when `MENU_ITEMS` is 8; `roadmap.md` said `SET1` v7 leaves "v1–v6 still decode" (v3–v6) and carried three stale page/metric counts against a `Page` enum that is now 41 built-ins plus four composed seats and a `face::Metric` catalogue that is now 40; and `settings_menu.rs`'s own doc comment priced the per-page mask at "33 checkboxes" against 45 pages, so it is now count-free. `tier1_log.md`'s §374 entry said `decode` accepts "v1–v7", which was wrong the day it was written rather than merely dated — it carries a bracketed correction rather than a rewrite.
- [x] **`body_metrics.weight_kg` has a reachable `23514`.** — **CLOSED 2026-08-29** ([decisions § 792](../architecture/decisions.md)). All bounds re-measured by replaying every migration; `weight_kg`, `target_percent_1rm` and `servings` are exactly as filed. **Three things the entry had wrong.** There is no `checkpoint_health` table — the weigh-in column is `checkpoint_crossings.body_weight_kg` (`between 20 and 400`, `20270201_001:71`); `checkpoint_health` names a *retention function*. `gym_routine_sets.target_percent_1rm` is listed as unbounded on the client and has **no client input surface at all** — no editor on either platform writes it, so there is nothing to bound. And the `min="0"` half is not reachable on the preferences card: the save path already coerces a non-positive value to null. **Four reachable raw errors were found, not one**: Settings › Preferences on BOTH fields (its `min`/`max` are decorative — the card saves from a button's `onclick`, not a form submit, so the height field's existing `max="300"` was worth nothing, the issue #677 trap again); the race-board weigh-in on both platforms, unbounded against 20..400, whose web label also read the unit off the DISTANCE preference while the parser read it off the WEIGHT one; recipe servings on mobile only (`parseTypedDecimal('0')` is `0.0`, not null — web was already clamped); and the parkrun number on web. `recipes.servings` also carries a `22003`, not a `23514`: its CHECK has no ceiling but `numeric(5,1)` does. All closed with the bound, the inline message and the i18n keys in seven web locales and seven ARBs, plus a Playwright case and two Flutter ones; the bounds themselves live once per client in `core/column_limits.ts` / `column_limits.dart`, keyed by `<table>.<column>`, and `check_shared_constants.mjs` proves each sits inside its column's own CHECK.
- [x] **`nutrition_targets`' sanity caps are a third home of two DB caps, inside an enforced parity pair.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 819](../architecture/decisions.md)). The premise is exactly right, and the mutation proves it: `body_metrics.weight_kg` is `> 0 and <= 500` and `user_profiles.height_cm` is `> 0 and <= 300`, both in `20261216_001`, so the pair's two literals were transcriptions — and widening the weight CHECK to 600 in a scratch migration set makes the existing containment guard raise **zero** errors about that column, because 250 is still inside 600. `COLUMN_CHECK_MAX` / `kColumnCheckMax` now holds what the COLUMN accepts, keyed the same `<table>.<column>`, and its guard demands **equality** with the CHECK it resolves by replay — refusing a cap below it (a stored value discarded as non-physical) as loudly as one above it, refusing an exclusive `< 500` rather than reading it as inclusive, and refusing a column whose CHECK has been dropped. Eight guard cases, six of them mutations. **Three things the filing had wrong.** The mirror-test count is **36**, not 34 — 18 each, now 19 each. **`ageYears > 120` is not a DB cap at all**: there is no age column, age is derived from `date_of_birth` and no CHECK bounds it, so it stays a stated ceiling — but it was written twice in the same file on both platforms and is now named once. And `column_limits`' own comment, written by § 792, said `height_cm` "allows anything above 0"; it caps at 300 and the registered client bound is EQUAL to it, not narrower. **The Health Connect clamp is worse than "unregistered"**: its 30–300 kg disagreed with the registered bound in BOTH directions — 300 is above the 250 the body-metrics field offers, so an import could seed a weight the runner then could not re-save, and 30 is above the 20 floor, so a light runner's real sample was dropped as noise. Knowingly left: the watch's `nutrition_targets.rs` keeps its literals, a one-way research-tier port with no database in reach. Original filing below.
  - Original: **`nutrition_targets`' sanity caps are a third home of two DB caps, inside an enforced parity pair.** `nutrition_targets.ts` / `nutrition_targets.dart` both refuse a stored profile with `weightKg > 500 || heightCm > 300 || ageYears > 120` — a defensive filter over data read BACK, so its numbers are the *column's* caps rather than the client input bounds `column_limits` now holds (250 / 300). The 500 and the 300 are transcriptions of `body_metrics.weight_kg` and `user_profiles.height_cm`; a CHECK widened to 600 would leave a legitimately-stored weight silently producing no calorie target on both platforms, with 34 mirror tests passing. Not closed in § 792 because the pair is under the `shared-library-syncer` lockstep and the fix wants a DB-cap accessor (`columnCheckMax`) that `column_limits` deliberately does not expose today — it holds what the CLIENT may send, not what the column accepts. Same shape, unregistered: `health_connect_importer.dart`'s "clamps to a sane 30–300 kg" on the imported body weight.
- [x] **`nutrition/recipes.spec.ts` fails whenever the seed's dinner falls inside the browser's UTC "today".** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** (`docs/testing/testing.md § Owning the day`; no new ADR — [§ 728](../architecture/decisions.md) already owns the class and this is its testing-guide rule). The shape and the cause are exactly as filed. **The window is not.** The seed's LATEST meal is `now() - 1 hour`, so the count is 2 only when the seed ran inside the **00:00–01:00 UTC hour** — 3 from 01:00, 4 from 04:00, 5 from 05:00 and **6 from 08:00 on**, which is most of the day rather than a six-hour window. Measured live against the local stack at 17:14 UTC: `Expected: 2, Received: 6`. The count is also not the only casualty — `ings.length`, the summed `calories` 250 and `protein_g` 25 all move with it. **And the mechanism is not § 728's.** `seed.sql` writes `now() - interval`, a `timestamptz` the DATABASE computes, so nothing is written in the runner's zone and no conversion happens: the seeded rows are on the browser's day legitimately and simply are not the spec's. Same family, different half. Fixed by taking BOTH offered shapes, because the calorie assertions need exclusivity and the ingredient assertion needs to say what it means: the spec clears the day through `browserDayStart()` the way the sibling `meal-templates.spec.ts` already does, seeds at fixed hours on the browser's calendar day, and names its two `item_name`s instead of counting. `fixtures/dates.test.ts` gains the rule — a spec driving `save-as-meal` or `save-as-recipe` must clear the day first — proved to name `recipes.spec.ts` against the pre-fix source. **A sweep of all 466 specs (≈90 read line by line) found no second genuine collision**, one fragile near-miss (`challenges/pace.spec.ts`, whose window admits the seed's own morning run but whose assertions are qualitative enough not to flip), and three disciplined scopings already in wide use. Original filing below.
  - Original: **`nutrition/recipes.spec.ts` fails whenever the seed's dinner falls inside the browser's UTC "today".** `save ingredients as a recipe` seeds two `food_log` rows and asserts the saved recipe has `ingredient_count === 2`, but the recipe is built from **every** entry on the diary's current day — and `seed.sql` places its meals relative to `now()`, so between roughly 00:00 and 06:00 UTC the seeded "Salmon, sweet potato & broccoli" is also today and the count is 3. Measured on this branch and on `main` alike (decisions § 792); it is [§ 728](../architecture/decisions.md)'s browser-vs-runner calendar day one layer on, since `playwright.config.ts` pins the context to UTC while the seed is written in the runner's zone. Fix shape: seed the two rows at a fixed hour inside the browser's day through `fixtures/dates.ts` and assert on the two `item_name`s rather than on a count of the whole day, or delete the day's other rows in the fixture.
- [x] **The race-board weigh-in and the body-metrics card disagree about which weight unit label to show.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 820](../architecture/decisions.md)). The key, the seven ARB strings and the rename are all as filed. **"Cosmetic today" is not.** The label and the suffix sit in one `InputDecoration`, so a runner on lbs reads **"Body weight (kg)"** with an **"lbs"** suffix — two units in one field, one of them wrong, not a stale string beside a correct one. Renamed to `checkpointWeighInBodyWeight` and de-unitted in all seven catalogues, pinned by a widget test that reads the rendered decoration under a lbs preference and a source guard that parses all seven ARBs, so a translator restoring "(kg)" in one language alone fails the PR; both proved to fail against the old string. **The sweep the entry asked for: two contradictions repo-wide**, no key-only cases, ~35 benign fixed-unit labels, and both contradictions mobile-only (web's own weigh-in label already interpolates the unit). The second is `prefsSplitIntervalDefaultSubtitle` — "Default (1 km for running, 5 km for cycling)" — where `ActivityType.splitIntervalMetresFor` returns **1 mi / 5 mi** in imperial by deliberate design, so the row named the wrong unit for exactly the runners that default exists for. Fixed in the same round, placeholders in all seven catalogues, pinned by a widget test under a mi preference. Original filing below.
  - Original: **The race-board weigh-in and the body-metrics card disagree about which weight unit label to show.** § 792 fixed the web board, which read `getUnit() === 'mi' ? 'lbs' : 'kg'` for the LABEL while `parseWeight` read the separate `weight_unit` signal — so a runner on km + lbs was shown "kg" and parsed as lbs. The mobile weigh-in sheet's label key is `checkpointWeighInWeightKg`, which names kg in the key and now carries a `suffixText` from the real unit; the key should be renamed and its seven ARB strings de-kg'd so the label cannot contradict the suffix. Cosmetic today (the suffix is authoritative), but the string still says "kg" in every locale.

## Surfaced by the #789 test-coverage round (2026-08-31)

Found while writing the coverage in [decisions §§ 807-814](../architecture/decisions.md). These are the ones that need their own change rather than being closed in that round.

- [ ] **`check_production_env` and `env_isolation` are off the required gate.** Both live in `env-isolation.yml`, a separate path-filtered workflow, so the release-build env guard's own tests reach no required check on the PR that breaks them — [§ 775](../architecture/decisions.md)'s shape exactly, on the guard that would catch a live Stripe key or a `*.supabase.co` URL in a release build. They are recorded as reasoned `OFF_THE_GATE` entries in `check_ci_diagnostics.test.mjs` so the set cannot drift further, but recording is not gating. Closing it means folding both into a job the `CI gate` aggregator waits on, which also means relocating `env-example-scan` and deleting a workflow — a change to CI structure, not a test, which is why the round that found it did not take it.
- [x] **Three mobile dotenv gates read `dotenv.env[...]` with no try/catch, where their sibling fails closed.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([§ 822](../architecture/decisions.md)). The filing got the framing right and every specific wrong. Right: `main.dart` loads dotenv before `runApp` unconditionally, so an uninitialised read is unreachable in the app; and the asymmetry was real and worth closing. Wrong: none of the three named gates was missing a guard. `offRouteEscalationEnabled` and `adaptiveFitnessGateEnabled` are pure `String? → bool` parsers that never touch dotenv and cannot throw — the reads are at their call sites, and all five of those guarded already, three with `try/catch` and two with `dotenv.isInitialized &&` (equivalent: `NotInitializedError` is the only thing `dotenv.env` throws). `_weighInGate` has had its `try/catch` since `71a255845`. What was actually wrong is the *binding*: key plus guard spelled at five call sites in two idioms, two of them duplicates, and two of them private top-level getters inside screen files — so the test the filing asked for could not be written. Each flag now owns one binding module (`off_route_flag.dart` / `adaptive_fitness_flag.dart` / `weigh_in_flag.dart`, beside the existing `nearby_flag.dart`, mirroring web's `*_flag.ts` layer), the parse stays in its parity-pair file, and `env_flag_test.dart` pins all four answering `false` against an uninitialised dotenv plus a guard that a gate key read literally outside its own module fails the suite.
- [ ] **Two waits in `nutrition_screen_test.dart` are measured vacuous.** "The saved template/recipe to appear in the picker" spends zero loop turns: `find.text` matches the dialog's own `EditableText`, so the predicate is already true before the save. It is [§ 723](../architecture/decisions.md)'s class, and it survived that round's sweep. Left in place deliberately for now — `pumpUntil` cannot wait on a route transition under the fake clock, so every correct predicate risks a timeout instead, and the `store.rows.length == 2` wait that follows does spin and does cover the behaviour. Closing it means a predicate that can distinguish the dialog's text from the picker's, or a different wait primitive for route transitions.
- [x] **`LocalRunStore` and `LocalRouteStore` carry the same unserialised delete-vs-write shape [§ 821](../architecture/decisions.md) closed on `OfflineSyncStore`.** **CLOSED** 2026-08-31 — [§ 828](../architecture/decisions.md) + [§ 829](../architecture/decisions.md). The filing was right about the shape, the outcome and the reason it was worth its own slice, and it under-counted its own evidence twice. Its headline said twelve atomic-write call sites outside `OfflineSyncStore` while its own breakdown adds to **fourteen** (7 + 5 + 2); twelve is the count for the two stores it names in the title. The route-store outcome it measured reproduces exactly — a `delete` against an in-flight `save` is silently dropped, memory *and* disk keep the row, and a cold load keeps it too — and the run store does the same plus re-writes the index the delete had just pruned. What it missed is the sidecars, which are the worse half: `route_owner_tags.json` is read-modify-write with a `_ownerTagsTouched` ledger cleared by whichever write lands first, so eight overlapping `save` calls left as few as **two of eight** routes tagged, and an untagged route is drainable into another account on a shared device ([§ 67](../architecture/decisions.md)) — reproducible about one run in three. `watch_ingest_queue.dart` was taken too (the entry's own closing sentence names three stores): `setLastKnownOwner` is fired unawaited from both `main.dart` call sites, and its clear-vs-stamp race reproduces every time. And the `FileLock` the filing flagged "worth checking on the way past" turned out to exclude nothing at all — POSIX record locks are owned by the process, so two handles in one process, including the WorkManager isolate the lock named, both acquire (§ 829); it is gone, its `.lock` residue is swept, and an arch guard fails the suite if either store spells it again. § 821's chain fit and was lifted into `serialiseStoreWrite` in `core_models`, made re-entrant so a shared persist helper cannot deadlock behind its own caller. The recording path is deliberately **outside** it — pinned by a test that holds the chain open through a never-completed `Completer` and asserts a tick still lands.
- [x] **`gym_detail_visibility_test.dart` prints a `PathNotFoundException` on the double-tap case.** **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([§ 821](../architecture/decisions.md)). The filing got the exception shape and the L4 catch right, and the diagnosis backwards on both halves. It is **in the test**, not in shared store logic: `persist` installs the row in `rowsById` synchronously, so the visibility chip flips a frame before the atomic write lands, `pumpUntil` returned on it, and the temp-directory teardown then deleted that write's `.tmp` out from under its rename — nothing to do with the double tap (the `_actionBusy` guard holds, `updateCalls` is 1) and equally reachable from the single-tap case. And the temp path has been unique per write since `writeStringAtomic` shipped, so the collision the filing implies could not happen. The test now waits on the file, which also pins the offline-first durability the chip was wrongly credited with proving. **But the family the filing was reaching for is real, and two members were measured in production code**: `clear()` (the sign-out wipe) deletes every file in the directory including the `.tmp` of an in-flight write, producing exactly that `PathNotFoundException`; and a delete that overtakes an in-flight write to the same id leaves the row's file back on disk with `rowsById` saying it is gone, resurrecting it at the next cold-load with no exception at all. Both are fixed by serialising every directory-mutating entry point on one chain keyed on the directory, and both are pinned by tests that fail on the old path.
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-31** ([decisions § 815](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **The 23 `handler_envelope.test.ts` integration tests are still outside every mutation operator.** Closed with the entry it restates, in the § 815 round. The 785 / 0 / 0 figure it cites was exact and still is; the 23 was deno's ignored count and the runnable set was 22 (23 now, with the verify-token case § 815 added). The fix shape it proposed — "a neutered SERVED tree between the boot step and the test step" — was the right half and the wrong operator: a blanket neuter proves only that each case notices a dead function, which is the weaker claim § 788 already makes about the other 785, and it would have found none of the three non-discriminating cases that a per-gate mutation with declared spares did.

## Surfaced by the #789 test-coverage round 28 (2026-09-01)

Found while writing the coverage in [decisions §§ 832-851](../architecture/decisions.md). Reported rather than half-fixed: each needs its own change, and two of them need a decision before any code.

- [ ] **`catalogue_browse`'s accent fold diverges between web and mobile on 1,443 letters, and the parity registry cannot see it.** Web folds with `normalize('NFD')` + `\p{Diacritic}` strip, which reaches every canonically-decomposable letter in Unicode; the Dart twin spells the class out as a 19-entry table because Dart's core library has no normalisation, and that table stops at Latin Extended-A. Measured over the BMP, **1,443 letters are de-accented by web and left intact by mobile**: all 245 of Latin Extended Additional (the Vietnamese set — `Đèo Hải Vân` folds to `deo hai van` on web and `đeo hải van` on mobile), 340 Greek across both blocks, 57 Cyrillic, 91 Latin Extended-B including the pinyin tone letters (`ǎ`, `ǐ`), Vietnamese `Ơ`/`Ư`, and 7 spacing diacritics inside Latin-1 itself (`´`, `¨`, `¯`, `¸`, `·`, `` ` ``, `^`) that web deletes outright. Two consequences, both real: a reader typing `hai` reaches the segment on web and not on the phone, and `byName` compares folded names, so **the catalogue sorts differently on the two platforms**. The doc comment on the Dart side is accurate about *why* it is a table and silent about *how far* the table reaches. `check_parity_pair_registry.mjs` compares the two registries, not the two behaviours, so nothing fails. The durable fix is the one `age_grade_tables.dart` already models — **generate** the table from NFD rather than hand-maintaining it, commit it, and guard it against the generator — not to narrow web to match, which would delete working behaviour. The letters with no canonical decomposition (`ø`, `đ`, `ł`, `ß`, `æ`, …) must stay out of it either way: web keeps them too, and folding them would invent an equivalence NFD does not have.
- [x] **If Supabase Cloud ships storage-api's `protect_objects_delete` trigger, the export sweep silently stops deleting anything.** Closed 2026-09-01 ([decisions § 857](../architecture/decisions.md)) — **measured, and it is not a forward risk.** CLI 2.84.2 (CI's pin) starts storage-api **v1.44.11** and CLI 2.109.1 (the workstation) starts **v1.62.5**; `0055-prevent-direct-deletes.sql` is in **both**, so the trigger is on CI too and the escape GUC the § 839 test set was load-bearing there, not a no-op. It is also **statement-level**, so it fires on a DELETE matching no rows: the nightly job has been raising every night whether or not anything was stale, and because the DELETE precedes `expire_stale_export_jobs()` no `data_export_jobs` row was expired either. `20270703000002` sets `storage.allow_delete_query` transaction-locally — the precedent `20260927_001`'s orphan cleanup already set — and counts the stale objects surviving its own DELETE, raising if any do: a delete FILTERED rather than refused reports zero through `get diagnostics` and used to go on to mark the row `expired` while the archive was still there. The fixture no longer hands the function the GUC, so the suite drives the escape rather than supplying it. The one thing that remains is an owner call, filed below.
- [x] **`check_shared_constants.mjs` reads the migration text for the photo buckets' mime lists, which is a different claim from what the applied row says.** Closed 2026-09-01 ([decisions § 858](../architecture/decisions.md)). **Both rails kept — they make different claims and neither subsumes the other:** the script compares the migration text against `STRIPPABLE_IMAGE_MIME_TYPES` and `kStrippableImageMimeTypes`, which is four source files agreeing with each other and which no SQL assertion can reach; pgtap reads the applied `storage.buckets` rows, which no source read can. The applied-row assertions are held over a DERIVED population — every bucket accepting any `image/` type — so a fifth image bucket is held to § 557 the day it appears rather than the day someone extends a list of four names, they carry the null arm the old `@>`/`<@` filter silently excluded (`null @> array[…]` is null, so a bucket that lost its allowlist dropped out of its own check), and `image/heic` / `image/heif` are now named outright across every bucket. The source rail was hardened in the same change: it compares under `match: 'all'`, which has no key coverage check, so a bucket it failed to resolve simply stopped being compared while the other three still agreed — both that and a statement assigning the column in a shape it cannot read (which it used to skip, reporting the value from BEFORE the change) now throw.

## Surfaced by the #789 test-coverage round 29 (2026-09-01)

- [ ] **Decide whether the export retention sweep should delete `storage.objects` rows at all, and measure what happens to the bytes when it does.** [§ 857](../architecture/decisions.md) restored the sweep by setting storage-api's documented escape GUC, which is what `20260927_001`'s orphan-blob cleanup already does — but that trigger exists precisely because deleting the row is not deleting the object, and its own hint says so ("This prevents accidental data loss from orphaned objects"). `20260927_001` recorded the belief that "the actual blob bytes are reaped by the storage backend's background sweeper once the row is gone"; **that belief is stated nowhere else in this repo and was not measured.** If it is wrong, every Art 20 archive ever swept is still sitting in the object store, unreachable through the Storage API but not erased — a retention claim `docs/compliance/data-subject-rights.md` makes on our behalf. Measuring it needs a real project (list the bucket through the S3-compatible endpoint after a sweep), not more reading. The alternative shape is to move the sweep onto the Storage API — a job kind in the Go worker, which already writes the archive through that API and already holds the service key — leaving `expire_stale_export_jobs()` as the only SQL half. That is a structural change to a shipped retention path and to the cron schedule, which is why it was not taken with the fix: the fix restores a sweep that was deleting nothing at all, and it is strictly better than the state it replaced under either answer.
